--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The mission is to get robots into the UPPER black strip (near blue walls), not the lower black strip (near green walls). The champion uses a 2-state FSM: State 0 explores, transitions on gray floor (C=1); State 1 stops, transitions back when neighbors are below threshold (C=4). Score ~19967.

Key insight: The champion doesn't use color perception to distinguish blue vs green walls. The best approach should:
1. Move toward BLUE walls (B=8, clr=3 which is blue) to reach the upper strip
2. Once on black floor near blue walls, stop/stay
3. Use GoAwayColor from GREEN (B=9, clr=2) as alternative

Let me design machines:

**Strategy:**
- **GoToColor BLUE** (B=8, clr=3) is the core navigation - blue walls border the rewarded upper strip
- **Stop** (B=1) once in position to minimize score
- Use floor color + color perception to switch states

**Machine designs:**

1. **Champion (exact copy)** - survival
2-4. **Exploitation of champion** - small parameter tweaks
5-7. **GoToBlue + Stop** - simple 2-state with blue-seeking
8-10. **GoToBlue + GoAwayGreen + Stop** - 3-state
11-14. **Exploration variants**

For colors: clr=3 is blue (BLUE walls = rewarded), clr=2 is green (GREEN walls = penalized).

Let me build carefully valid FSMs:

For 2-state FSM with GoToColor blue:
- State 0: GoToColor blue (B=8, vel=1.0, clr=3)
- State 1: Stop (B=1)
- Transition 0→1: black floor (C=0, high prob) - reached black strip
- Transition 1→0: low neighbors (C=4) or perceive green (C=7, l=2) - wrong strip

## Reasoning

The champion (score 5493.73) is a 3-state FSM:
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE (B=8, clr=3) - moves toward blue walls
- State 1: Stop (B=1) - stays put once in position
- State 2: GoAwayColor GREEN (B=9, clr=2) - moves away from green walls

Transitions:
- S0→S1: black floor (p=0.9) OR sees blue (p=0.5)
- S1→S0: sees green (p=0.5) OR gray floor (p=0.7) OR random (p=0.05)
- S2→S0: sees blue (p=0.8) OR black floor (p=0.6)

This is excellent! The core logic: go toward blue, stop when on black (upper strip), escape if you detect green. 

**Plan:**
1. Copy champion exactly (survival)
2. Exploit champion: tweak probabilities slightly
3. Exploit runner-up 2 (score 6473): similar 3-state
4. Exploit runner-up 3 (score 6511): 4-state variant
5. Exploit runner-up 4 (score 6758): explore-then-navigate
6. Explore: add more transitions, different state topologies

Key improvements to try:
- Higher probability for black→stop transition
- Different thresholds for green/blue detection
- Add a 4th state for robustness
- Vary the random escape probability from stop state

## Reasoning

The champion (score 4063.68) is excellent. Let me analyze it carefully:

- **State 0**: GoToColor BLUE (B=8, clr=3) → Stop on black floor (p=0.9) OR sees GREEN (p=0.5, l=2) [note: l0x1=2 means GREEN]
- **State 1**: Stop → go to GoAwayGreen if sees green (p=0.5) OR gray floor (p=0.7) OR random (p=0.05)
- **State 2**: GoAwayColor GREEN (B=9, clr=2) → go to Stop if sees BLUE (p=0.8) OR black floor (p=0.6)

Key insight: In the champion, transition 0→1 uses `l0x1=2` (GREEN), meaning "if I see green while going to blue, stop". This seems counterintuitive but combined with state 2 escaping green, it works. The robot goes to blue, stops on black, and if it accidentally sees green it gets redirected.

**Exploitation plan**: Tweak probabilities around the champion's values, add/modify transitions.

**Key things to try**:
1. Increase black-floor→stop probability (currently 0.9 is already high)
2. Adjust the green-detection probabilities in states 1 and 2
3. Try adding a transition from S0: if sees BLUE directly, go to stop
4. Try higher/lower p for gray-floor escape from stop
5. Explore: add 4th state as backup explorer

Let me be careful: `l` values - clr=2 is GREEN (bad), clr=3 is BLUE (good). LED colours must be 0,4,5,6.

## Reasoning

The champion (4063.68) is stable. The top 5 performers this round all score ~4063-4306, very close together. The topology is converging on:
- S0: GoToBlue → Stop on black (p≈0.9) OR sees green (p≈0.5)
- S1: Stop → GoAwayGreen if sees green OR gray floor OR random small prob
- S2: GoAwayGreen → Stop if sees blue OR black floor

Key observations:
1. Adding an extra transition in S2 (sees blue, l=3) seems to help slightly (rank 1 this round: 4200.76)
2. Higher gray-floor prob in S1 (0.75 vs 0.7) seems slightly worse
3. The `l0x1=2` (sees GREEN while in GoToBlue → Stop) is consistently good
4. 4-state machines with explorer state are worse
5. Very tight parameter space - need fine-grained search

**Exploitation strategy**: 
- Keep the core 3-state topology
- Vary p0x0 (black→stop): try 0.87, 0.91, 0.93
- Vary p1x0 (sees green from stop): try 0.4, 0.45, 0.55
- Vary p1x1 (gray floor from stop): try 0.6, 0.68, 0.72
- Vary p2x0 (sees blue from GoAwayGreen): try 0.7, 0.85, 0.9
- Vary p2x1 (black floor from GoAwayGreen): try 0.5, 0.55, 0.65
- Try adding 3rd transition to S2 (sees blue, l=3) as in rank 1
- Try combining features from ranks 1+2

**Exploration**: Try a 4-state with duplicate GoToBlue states for different contexts.

## Reasoning

The champion (3867.88) has been stable for 2 rounds. The search is converging tightly. Key findings:

**What works:**
- p0x0=0.87 is optimal (0.85 and 0.86 worse)
- p0x1=0.52 is optimal (0.50 and 0.54 slightly worse)
- p1x0=0.5 (green→GoAwayGreen from Stop)
- p1x1=0.7 (gray→GoAwayGreen from Stop)
- p1x2=0.05 (random escape from Stop)
- p2x0=0.8 (sees-blue→Stop from GoAwayGreen)
- p2x1=0.6 (black→Stop from GoAwayGreen)
- p2x2=0.5 (sees-blue again→Stop from GoAwayGreen)

**What doesn't help:**
- p1x0=0.48 (worse)
- p2x0=0.85 with p2x1=0.55 (worse)
- p0x0=0.85 (worse)

**Strategy for round 7:**
- Keep champion exact copy
- Fine-tune around exact champion values with very small perturbations
- Try p0x0 in {0.86, 0.87, 0.88, 0.89}
- Try p0x1 in {0.51, 0.52, 0.53}
- Try p2x1 in {0.58, 0.6, 0.62}
- Try p1x1 in {0.68, 0.70, 0.72}
- Try adding 4th transition to S1: black floor → GoAwayGreen (to escape lower strip)
- Explore: try S0 also checking for gray to detect mid-arena

## Reasoning

The champion (3867.88) is still holding. Key findings from round 8:

**What's working slightly better than champion:**
- p1x1=0.68 (rank 1: 4136) and p1x1=0.69 (rank 2: 4147) vs champion's 0.7
- p2x1=0.56 with p1x1=0.68 (rank 1: 4136)
- p1x0=0.48 with p1x1=0.68 (rank 4: 4189)

**What's worse:**
- p1x2=0.04 consistently worse than 0.05
- p2x0=0.78 worse
- Combining too many changes at once dilutes signal

**Key insight:** The scores are all ~4100-4460, still above champion's 3867. The champion seems to be at a local optimum. The variance in simulation is significant - the champion's score of 3867 might be partly luck. 

**Strategy for round 9 (penultimate):**
1. Keep champion exact copy
2. Combine the best findings: p1x1=0.68, p2x1=0.56 (rank 1 combination)
3. Try p1x1=0.68 with p2x1=0.55, 0.57
4. Try p1x1=0.69 with various p2x1
5. Try p1x0=0.48 combined with p1x1=0.68 and p2x1=0.56
6. Try a broader exploration: different p0x0 values (0.83, 0.84) with the new p1x1=0.68 structure
7. Try slightly different p2x2 values

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. At every simulation step the loop function adds +1 to the score for each robot that is NOT inside the target region. The score is MINIMISED, so a lower score means the swarm reached the region earlier and more robots stayed in it.

THE TARGET REGION is the upper strip of the arena. It is NOT identified by a unique floor colour: the floor is BLACK in BOTH the upper and the lower strip, and GRAY in between. Only the UPPER black strip is rewarded. Sitting on the lower black strip scores exactly as badly as standing in the middle of the arena.

HOW TO TELL THE TWO STRIPS APART. At the start of the run the arena walls light up: the two walls bordering the REWARDED upper strip are BLUE, the two walls bordering the penalised lower strip are GREEN. Colour is the only reliable cue.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. A black-floor condition is useful to detect that a strip has been reached, but on its own it sends half the swarm to the wrong strip. It must be combined with colour-driven motion - move toward BLUE, or move away from GREEN - to reach the correct one. Once inside, robots should stay rather than keep exploring, since every step spent outside costs a point. Clustering tightly is NOT rewarded: only being inside counts.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 6 walls, inner radius 0.80 m. 21 e-puck robots start scattered uniformly in a square of half-width 0.78 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.54 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
